## Comprehensive Exploration Report: agent-skills-shaper

### 1. SKILL.md Required Structure and Frontmatter

From `docs/skill-anatomy.md`, here is the **exact required frontmatter specification**:

```yaml
---
name: <skill-name>
description: <1-2 sentences, includes trigger phrases>
pack: <product | engineering | meta>
lifecycle_stage: <define | plan | build | ship | verify | review | meta | reference>
principles_implemented:
  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product>
    id: <principle or rule id>
    bucket: <embedded | standalone | hook | sub-agent>
length_target: <e.g. 200-280>
author: Anton Babushkin
predecessor:
  repo: <URL or "none">
  skill: <skill name in source repo, or "none">
  relation: <derivative | adjacent | new>
kept_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
changed_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
---
```

**Required sections in exact order:**

1. **Title** — `# <Skill Name>`
2. **Purpose** — one paragraph
3. **When to use** — explicit trigger phrases and intents, with the size-the-work rubric where relevant
4. **When not to use** — explicit non-triggers, with the right alternative skill named
5. **Inputs** — what the skill expects to be given
6. **Outputs** — what it produces, including the artefact path under `docs/`
7. **Workflow** — numbered steps with hard gates marked `[GATE]`
8. **Artefact template** — the markdown the skill produces
9. **Common rationalisations** — table of typical excuses and rebuttals
10. **Red flags** — bullet list of signs the skill is being violated
11. **Verification / exit criteria** — how to know the skill ran correctly
12. **References** — pointers to `references/` and to source principles

**Length targets:** 100–300 lines. Below 100 lines faces editor scrutiny for under-specification; above 300 lines faces editor scrutiny for redundancy, scope creep, or over-explanation. Hard cap: 350 lines; beyond that, split the skill.

**Note on artefact template section naming:** Skills that produce a single named artefact may use descriptive variant names (e.g., "Task list template", "Finding template") instead of generic "Artefact template" — the name identifies the artefact type, but the section must still occupy the exact position in the required sequence.

**Meta-pack skills exemption:** Skills with `pack: meta` are navigation/orientation artefacts, not workflow skills. They are exempt from: `principles_implemented` frontmatter, `predecessor` frontmatter, and these body sections: Inputs, Outputs, Artefact template, Common rationalisations, Red flags, and Verification / exit criteria. They must still include: Title, Purpose, When to use, When not to use, and References or equivalent lookup table.

**Rules files note:** Root `PRODUCT_RULES.md`, `eng-principles-universal.md`, and `eng-principles-agentic.md` mirror the shape of their source principles documents, not the anatomy template.

---

### 2. Complete docs/ Directory Listing

| File | One-line summary |
|------|------------------|
| `authoring-learnings.md` | Calibration guidance and failure modes for skill authoring |
| `benchmarks.md` | Methodology and results for the plan-review skill (93% pass rate vs 19% baseline on Sonnet 4.6) |
| `brief-template.md` | Template for the brief that drives skill authoring projects |
| `hook-anatomy.md` | Authoring spec for deterministic mechanical hooks (`hooks/<name>/HOOK.md`) |

| `skill-anatomy.md` | This document — the full required structure of SKILL.md files |

### 3. Complete references/ Directory Listing

These are **shared reference files** — lookup tables and taxonomies cited by multiple skills:

| File | One-line summary | Cited by |
|------|------------------|----------|
| `app-context-schema.md` | JSON schema for app context files; defines validity rules for `docs/app-context.md` | app-calibrate skill |
| `confidence-meter.md` | Itamar Gilad's 5-level evidence quality scale (Opinion, Anecdote, Indirect, Experiment, Validated); maps 1:1 to ICE Confidence dimension | idea-triage skill; PRODUCT_RULES.md |
| `dora-metrics.md` | Definitions of DORA KPIs (deployment frequency, lead time, MTTR, change failure rate) | Referenced by engineering principles and roadmap planning |
| `ice-scoring.md` | ICE formula (Impact × Confidence × Ease) for comparing product hypotheses; includes Confidence Meter integration and routing logic | idea-triage skill; PRODUCT_RULES.md |
| `initiative-types.md` (363 lines) | Six-type taxonomy for classifying Linear initiatives by success definition (Type 1–6) | initiative-shape skill; linear-workflow.md |
| `kano-classification.md` | Five-category taxonomy for feature types by user satisfaction response (Must-have, Linear, Delighter, Indifferent, Dissatisfier) | idea-triage skill |
| `kr-quality-templates.md` (213 lines) | Key result templates and quality checks for different initiative types | initiative-shape skill |
| `nfr-categories.md` | Taxonomy of non-functional requirements (Performance, Scalability, Security, Reliability, Maintainability, etc.) with unit/measurement guidance | design-doc skill (Rule A4) |
| `portfolio-themes.md` | Shreyas Doshi's seven-theme taxonomy for classifying roadmap items by strategic purpose (Differentiators, Table-stakes, Incrementals, Embarrassments, Customer specials, Tech foundation, Speculative bets) | roadmap-shape skill |

**Pattern:** References follow a consistent frontmatter format with `type: reference`, `name:`, `description:`, and critically, a **`cited_by:`** field listing which skills and rules consume them. This creates a bidirectional contract: the reference documents are explicit dependencies, and they are version-stable lookup tables — not ephemeral explanations.

---

### 4. How Skills Cite Shared References

**Example citation format from `skills/planning-and-task-breakdown/SKILL.md`:**

```markdown
## References

- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — Rule A1 (design doc trigger), Rule B1 (YAGNI per slice), Rule B2 (walking skeleton first), Rule C4 (dependencies before commitment)
- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P3 (no drift from design doc)
- `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` — the upstream output that this skill consumes
- Ryan Singer — "Shape Up" (appetite and scope as planning primitives)
```

**Example from `skills/backlog-manage/SKILL.md`:**

```markdown
- `references/confidence-meter.md` — Gilad's scale for evidence quality scoring
- `references/ice-scoring.md` — ICE = Impact × Confidence × Ease mechanics
```

**Example from `skills/app-calibrate/SKILL.md`:**

```markdown
Both modes produce a file that satisfies the validity rules defined in `references/app-context-schema.md`.
```

The pattern is: backticks around the relative path (always starting from repo root), followed by a dash and a brief description of what from that document is being used. Citations are precise — they name the specific principle/rule ID or section being invoked, not just the whole document.

### 5. Plugin Registration and Command Discovery

**plugin.json structure** (`.claude-plugin/plugin.json`):

```json
{
  "name": "shape",
  "description": "Workflow skills for small product-design-engineering teams...",
  "version": "0.1.0",
  "author": { "name": "Anton Babushkin" },
  "homepage": "https://github.com/ababushkin/agent-skills-shaper",
  "repository": "https://github.com/ababushkin/agent-skills-shaper",
  "license": "MIT",
  "commands": "./.claude/commands",
  "hooks": "./hooks/hooks.json"
}
```

**Registration mechanism: AUTO-DISCOVERY.** Skills are NOT manually registered anywhere. Instead:

1. **Commands:** The `commands` field points to `./.claude/commands/`, a directory of `.md` files — one per command. Each command file references its corresponding SKILL.md:

   ```markdown
   ---
   description: Run planning-and-task-breakdown — decomposes a design doc or spec into small, verifiable tasks...
   argument-hint: "[design doc or spec]"
   ---

   @../../skills/planning-and-task-breakdown/SKILL.md

   Run the planning-and-task-breakdown skill for the following: $ARGUMENTS
   ```

   The `@` syntax creates an explicit reference to the skill file, and the convention appears to be: one `.md` file per command in `./.claude/commands/`, named after the skill (e.g., `planning-and-task-breakdown.md`). No central registry — the directory itself is the registry.

2. **Hooks:** The `hooks` field points to `./hooks/hooks.json`, which presumably lists hook configurations.

3. **Sub-agent personas:** A `./.claude/agents/` directory exists with example files like `entire-search.md`, which use a simplified frontmatter with `model: haiku` specified. These are not auto-discovered in the same way; they appear to be manually placed.

**Conclusion:** New skills require:
- A `SKILL.md` in `skills/<skill-slug>/`
- A corresponding `.md` file in `./.claude/commands/<skill-slug>.md` pointing to the SKILL.md
- No changes to plugin.json or any registry

### 6. Model Selection and Agent Persona Concepts

**Finding: Model selection is present but minimal in the current repo.**

**Evidence:**

1. **Sub-agent anatomy mentions model choice:**
   - `./.claude/agents/entire-search.md` includes `model: haiku` in its frontmatter.
   - This is the ONLY evidence of per-agent model specification in the main codebase.

2. **Benchmarks document model choices for skill evaluation:**
   - `docs/benchmarks.md` explicitly compares Claude Sonnet 4.6 vs Opus 4.7:
   
   > "Iter-4 was Opus 4.7 on both runner and grader; iter-5 was Sonnet 4.6 on both. The +0.74 vs iter-4's +0.54 numbers are not apples-to-apples — the sonnet baseline produces less structured output (without-skill score dropped 36% → 19%), which the strict grader penalises more heavily."

   - The document notes that iter-5 achieved ~30% token cost reduction by switching from Opus to Sonnet while maintaining the +0.74 delta.
   - However, this is **research/benchmarking context**, not a runtime routing decision.

3. **No evidence of Haiku/Sonnet/Opus selection at skill level:**
   - Skills themselves contain **zero** model selection hints or constraints.
   - The engineering principles documents mention no model-specific tuning.
   - The frontmatter spec in `skill-anatomy.md` has no `model:` field.

4. **Sub-agent personas have a dedicated concept:**
   - `docs/sub-agent-anatomy.md` defines sub-agents as "review passes invoked with fresh context" — these are explicit reviewer personas with their own naming, frontmatter, and lifecycle.
   - They have `review_target:` (design-doc, implementation, spec, other) and `bucket: sub-agent` in frontmatter.
   - They are the mechanism for "another agent" checks, not model selection.

**Conclusion:** 
- **Sub-agent persona** is a first-class concept: a review pass with a specific, named posture (adversarial, fresh-eyes). These can optionally have a `model:` field (as seen in `entire-search.md`), but it is not required or commonly used in the current skill set.
- **Model selection** exists for research/benchmarking (Sonnet vs Opus trade-off documented) but is not part of the skill authoring contract.
- **Haiku, Sonnet, Opus** are mentioned only in `benchmarks.md` for cost-performance discussion, not as a runtime concept.
- **Runtime/agent model assignment** is NOT present in the skill architecture.

---

### Summary of Authoring Contracts

**For a new SKILL.md:**
- Provide full frontmatter (12 required fields)
- 12 required body sections in strict order
- Target 100–300 lines
- Cite shared references by relative path with specific principle/rule IDs
- No model selection in the skill definition

**For a new reference file (`references/X.md`):**
- Provide `type: reference` in frontmatter
- Include `cited_by:` field listing consuming skills
- Maintain bidirectional traceability
- Keep stable — it's a shared lookup table, not prose

**For a new sub-agent persona (`agents/<name>/AGENT.md`):**
- 8 required sections (see `sub-agent-anatomy.md`)
- Optional `model:` field in frontmatter
- Review posture and context-loading are non-negotiable
- Target 150–200 lines

**For new commands:**
- Create `./.claude/commands/<skill-slug>.md`
- Point to the skill with `@` syntax
- Provide description and argument-hint
- No plugin.json edit needed
```

Yes — there are three relevant ones, but only one is a true fit for the "brainstorm and refine" phase you asked for:

**Best fit — `agent-skills:idea-refine`** (the addyosmani pack, not Shaper). It's purpose-built for exactly this: "Refine ideas through structured divergent and convergent thinking." That's the right tool for the stage we're at right now — taking your raw idea (systematic sizing + model selection) and pressure-testing it before it becomes a spec.

**Wrong fit despite the name — `shape-idea-triage`** (this pack). It looks relevant ("intake gate for new ideas"), but it's built for *product* ideas: it frames a customer problem, scores ICE, classifies Kano, and routes to build/validate/defer. Our idea is a **pack-internal authoring capability**, not a customer-facing feature — so the ICE/Kano machinery would be forcing a square peg. It's the wrong gate for meta-work on the pack itself.

**For later, not now — `skill-creator`** (its own pack). This is the build-phase tool for when we actually author the new skill(s)/reference(s). Not a refinement tool.

My recommendation: I'll use the **divergent → convergent** structure from `idea-refine` to frame our brainstorm here in-thread, rather than formally invoking the skill (it's interactive and would fight with plan mode). Same discipline, no ceremony.

Before I diverge — one thing the exploration surfaced that sharpens the whole idea. Your pack already has a hard stance that's directly in tension with part of your request:

> **eng-principles-agentic Principle 8:** "Effort is measured in **slices and gates**, not in calendar time... asking an agent to produce 'time estimates' forces fabrication."

And `planning-and-task-breakdown` reinforces it: *"size is not about effort estimation; it is about verification granularity."*

So your instinct — "estimate the size of the task" — is right, but the pack will reject any sizing that smells like hours/days/story-points. The interesting design question is: **what are the units of size for an agent-built task?** Your three factors (operator review load, hallucination risk, domain complexity) are actually the answer hiding in the question — they're *risk* dimensions, not *effort* dimensions. That reframe is the core of the brainstorm.

Let me lay out the divergent space, then I'll ask you a few framing questions.

## Divergent: what could this capability be?

**The two things you asked for, named:**

1. **Model/runtime selector** — "which agent runs this?" (Claude-only for now, but the *slot* should exist: Haiku/Sonnet/Opus, or a named sub-agent persona).
2. **Risk-weighted sizer** — not "how big," but "how likely is this to go wrong, and how hard is it to catch when it does?" Your three factors map cleanly:
   - **Review cognitive load** → how expensive is it for the operator to verify the artefact? (a one-line config change vs. a 400-line state machine)
   - **Hallucination/mistake risk** → how much does this depend on facts the agent will confidently fabricate? (novel API surface, version-specific behaviour — ties directly to agentic Principle 2)
   - **Domain complexity** → how likely is scope drift or a missed failure case? (ties to agentic Principle 3, "shortest path is the failure mode")

**Where it could live (these aren't exclusive):**

- **(A) A shared reference** — `references/task-sizing.md` defining the rubric + model-selection guidance once, the way `ice-scoring.md` and `confidence-meter.md` define their rubrics once and get cited by many skills. This is the DRY backbone regardless of what else we build.
- **(B) New fields threaded into existing artefact templates** — add a sizing/model annotation to the task-list template in `planning-and-task-breakdown`, the initiative template in `initiative-shape`, maybe the roadmap entries. (The exploration confirmed none of these have such a field today.)
- **(C) A new standalone skill** — e.g. `task-sizing` or `agent-routing` that takes a task list and annotates each item. Choke-point model.
- **(D) A new hook** — mechanical check that flags any task/initiative lacking a size+model annotation (like `stop-the-line` flags suppressions).

**The insight worth stealing from your own pack:** you already have a working pattern for "a 1–10 score with a named rubric per level" (ICE) and "a 5-level evidence ladder" (Confidence Meter). A risk-sizer could be a **3-axis score**, one axis per factor, each with a named rubric — and the *model selection falls out of the score* (high hallucination risk → Opus + source-driven-development; low everything → Haiku is fine). That makes model-selection a *consequence* of sizing, not a separate manual choice.

Before I converge this into options, three framing questions.

1	---
2	name: ice-scoring
3	description: >
4	  ICE scoring mechanics — Impact, Confidence, Ease dimensions and worked example.
5	  Used for prioritising validated hypotheses.
6	type: reference
7	cited_by:
8	  - skills/idea-triage/SKILL.md
9	  - rules/PRODUCT_RULES.md
10	---
11	
12	# ICE scoring
13	
14	ICE is a lightweight scoring method for comparing product hypotheses against each other. The formula is Impact × Confidence × Ease. Sean Ellis coined it; Itamar Gilad incorporated it into the GIST framework. It produces a relative priority number, not an absolute measure of value — a score of 200 in one team's backlog tells you nothing about a 200 in another team's. The Confidence dimension is the most important of the three because it is the hardest to inflate unconsciously: it requires naming actual evidence, not just asserting that the problem matters.
15	
16	## Dimensions
17	
18	| Dimension | Question it answers | Score (1–10) |
19	|---|---|---|
20	| Impact | If this works, how much does it move the metric that matters? | 1 = negligible, 10 = transformative |
21	| Confidence | How strong is the evidence that the problem is real and the solution will work? | Calibrated using the Confidence Meter (see `references/confidence-meter.md`) |
22	| Ease | How hard is this to build and ship? | 1 = very hard, 10 = trivial |
23	
24	## Formula and score interpretation
25	
26	**ICE = I × C × E**
27	
28	Maximum possible score: 1000. That number is not meaningful on its own.
29	
30	Use ICE to compare options within the same context — same team, same planning cycle, same outcome focus. Do not compare scores across teams or product areas; the inputs are too context-dependent.
31	
32	Ease is the least reliable dimension at triage stage. Treat it as a rough proxy, not a real estimate. It can be sharpened later with a walking skeleton or technical spike (see `eng-principles-universal.md` Rule B2), but at triage the number is a direction, not a commitment.
33	
34	## Worked example
35	
36	**Idea:** Add an in-app onboarding checklist for new users.
37	
38	| Dimension | Score | Rationale |
39	|---|---|---|
40	| Impact | 7 | New user activation is a top-of-funnel metric with direct revenue link; meaningful if it works |
41	| Confidence | 3 | Indirect data — user research shows confusion during onboarding, but no experiment has been run |
42	| Ease | 5 | Moderate — checklist UI exists in the component library; backend state management is needed |
43	| **ICE** | **105** | 7 × 3 × 5 |
44	
45	Routing: Confidence 3 is below the threshold of 5, so this idea goes to a validation slot, not a build slot. The next step is a targeted experiment or smoke test to raise confidence before any build commitment is made.
46	
47	## What ICE is not for
48	
49	**Replacing judgment.** ICE surfaces what deserves discussion; it does not replace the discussion. Two ideas with scores of 200 and 210 are not meaningfully different — argue about the underlying assumptions, not the decimal.
50	
51	**Comparing fundamentally different bet types.** A growth experiment and a platform investment both get ICE scores, but those scores do not belong on the same scale. Apples-to-oranges comparisons belong in a portfolio allocation conversation (Rule B3), not an ICE sort.
52	
53	**Validating the idea.** A high ICE score on an opinion-level hypothesis is a high-confidence guess. It does not mean the idea is good; it means someone scored their own assumptions generously. Score the Confidence dimension honestly against actual evidence, using the Confidence Meter. If the evidence is thin, the Confidence score is low — and that is the right answer.
54	
55	## Sources
56	
57	- Sean Ellis — coined ICE scoring
58	- Itamar Gilad — incorporated into GIST Planning; Confidence Meter integration
59	- `references/confidence-meter.md` — Confidence dimension calibration
60	
```

1	---
2	name: confidence-meter
3	description: >
4	  Calibration scale for evidence quality, used when scoring the Confidence dimension in ICE.
5	  Source: Itamar Gilad.
6	type: reference
7	cited_by:
8	  - skills/idea-triage/SKILL.md
9	  - rules/PRODUCT_RULES.md
10	---
11	
12	# Confidence Meter
13	
14	The Confidence Meter is Itamar Gilad's framework for scoring the quality of evidence behind a product hypothesis. It appears in his "GIST Planning" framework and the associated "Confidence Meter" blog post. The scale measures evidence quality — not how strongly someone believes something. A team can hold a conviction at maximum certainty while the underlying evidence is still opinion-level. The distinction matters because it drives routing: low-evidence ideas warrant validation work, not build bets. High-evidence ideas have earned consideration for a build slot.
15	
16	## The scale
17	
18	| Level | Score | What it means | Example |
19	|---|---|---|---|
20	| Opinion | 0.1 | Assumption, gut feel, executive intuition — no external data | "Our CEO thinks customers want this" |
21	| Anecdote | 0.5 | One or a few informal observations; not systematically gathered | "A sales rep heard this from two customers" |
22	| Indirect data | 2–5 | Surveys, market research, user interviews — structured but not causal | "70% of survey respondents said this is painful" |
23	| Experiment | 5–8 | Smoke tests, A/B tests, prototypes with measured outcomes — causal signal | "Smoke test showed 40% click-through on the fake door" |
24	| Validated | 8–10 | Launched feature with sustained behavioural data; causal effect observed | "Cohort analysis shows 15% retention lift after 90 days" |
25	
26	## Applying the scale to ICE
27	
28	The Confidence score feeds directly into the ICE Confidence dimension. The Gilad scale maps 1:1 to the ICE 1–10 range — Gilad 0.1 becomes ICE Confidence 1; Gilad 8–10 becomes ICE Confidence 8–10. No conversion formula is needed; the ranges map naturally. Assign the score that reflects the best quality of evidence actually in hand, not the evidence the team hopes to gather.
29	
30	## Common miscalibrations
31	
32	**Treating strong conviction as high confidence.** Confidence is about evidence quality, not certainty of belief. A highly articulate argument backed by no data is still 0.1. The persuasiveness of the argument is irrelevant to the score.
33	
34	**Treating "we've seen this pattern before" as indirect data.** Recollection of past patterns without documented evidence is an anecdote (0.5), not market data. Memory of a pattern is not a survey; recalling a past experiment is not the experiment itself.
35	
36	**Averaging scores across evidence types.** If you have one user interview and one survey, the score is not an average. Take the best evidence type available — the survey sets the floor at 2–5. Blending a 0.5 anecdote with a 3 survey to get 1.75 understates the confidence you actually have.
37	
38	## Routing implication
39	
40	In idea-triage, the Gilad score drives the routing gate: below 5 routes to a validation slot; at or above 5 routes to the idea bank as a build candidate. This document does not encode the routing logic — see `skills/idea-triage/SKILL.md` for the full routing workflow.
41	
42	## Sources
43	
44	- Itamar Gilad, "Confidence Meter" (blog post)
45	- Itamar Gilad, "GIST Planning" framework
46	- `references/ice-scoring.md` — ICE scoring mechanics and worked examples
47	
```

44	---
45	
46	# Planning and task breakdown
47	
48	## Purpose
49	
50	This skill takes an accepted design doc and produces an ordered task list where each task is small enough to complete and verify independently. It bridges the gap between "we have an accepted design" and "we know exactly what to build in what order." The output is a sequence of well-scoped tasks, each with acceptance criteria, that whichever build skill the team uses will pick up one at a time.
51	
52	The goal is not comprehensiveness — it is clarity and verifiability. A task list that looks thorough but whose tasks are too big to verify will not survive contact with implementation. Every task must answer: "how do I know this is done?"
53	
54	## When to use
55	
56	- A design doc has been accepted and implementation is ready to begin.
57	- A well-scoped spec exists for a change that doesn't meet the Rule A1 design-doc trigger, but is complex enough that sequencing and acceptance criteria are non-obvious.
58	
59	## When not to use
60	
61	- **No accepted design doc and the work exceeds a Rule A1 trigger** — run `design-doc` first. A draft or in-flight doc does not count as accepted.
62	- **The scope is unclear or contested** — resolve scope ambiguity in the design doc before breaking down tasks. Task breakdown from a vague spec produces tasks nobody believes in.
63	
64	## Inputs
65	
66	An accepted design doc covering at minimum: the problem being solved, the chosen approach, the components being changed or created, and the constraints. The design doc does not need to be perfect; it needs to be stable enough that the approach won't change during implementation.
67	
68	## Outputs
69	
70	An ordered task list at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`. Each task has: a one-sentence description, acceptance criteria (how you know it's done), and any upstream dependencies called out. The list begins with the walking skeleton as task 1.
71	
72	## Workflow
73	
74	**1. Read the design doc end-to-end.**
75	Do not start decomposing until you have read the full document. The most common task breakdown error is starting with the first section and ignoring the constraints and operability sections at the end. Those sections often constrain the sequencing.
76	
77	**2. Identify the walking skeleton.**
78	The walking skeleton is the thinnest possible slice that exercises the full path end-to-end — from input to output — without filling in the real functionality. It is always task 1. If you cannot identify a walking skeleton (a slice that makes the system run, however crudely), the design doc is missing something. Stop and resolve it before proceeding.
79	
80	Examples:
81	- API endpoint: a handler that accepts the request, returns a hardcoded response, passes a smoke test — no real logic yet.
82	- UI feature: a route that renders the page with static content — no data binding yet.
83	- Pipeline stage: a step that accepts the input format and emits the output format with placeholder data — no processing yet.
84	
85	**3. Identify the feature slices.**
86	Starting from the walking skeleton, list the increments that build toward the full design. Each increment should:
87	- Extend functionality in one direction (add real logic, add a field, add an error path)
88	- Be independently testable
89	- Leave the system in a working, deployable state
90	
91	Do not batch unrelated concerns into one slice. If a task touches the data layer AND the API AND the UI, it is three tasks.
92	
93	**4. [GATE] Write acceptance criteria for each task.**
94	Before ordering tasks, write the "done" check for each one. An acceptance criterion is a test, an assertion, or a measurable condition — not a description of what to build. If you cannot write the acceptance criterion, the task is not well enough understood to schedule.
95	
96	Format: `Task is done when: [specific, verifiable condition]`
97	
98	- Bad: "Task is done when the search feature is implemented."
99	- Good: "Task is done when: a query against the `/search` endpoint returns a 200 with the correct result shape for a known test input, covered by an automated integration test."
100	
101	**5. [GATE] Size check.**
102	Review every task. Any task that:
103	- Cannot be described in one sentence
104	- Has more than one acceptance criterion that requires a separate deployment to verify
105	- Cannot be completed and verified in a single uninterrupted implementation session — i.e. it bundles multiple independently verifiable changes (see `eng-principles-agentic.md` P8: size by slice count and verification granularity, not hours)
106	
107	is too big. Split it. Size is not about effort estimation; it is about verification granularity. Small tasks surface problems early.
108	
109	Example — too big:
110	> "Implement user authentication, including sign-up, login, password reset, and session management."
111	
112	Split into verifiable tasks:
113	> Task A: POST /auth/signup creates a user and returns a JWT — covered by an integration test.
114	> Task B: POST /auth/login returns a JWT for valid credentials, 401 for invalid — covered by tests.
115	> Task C: POST /auth/password-reset sends a reset email — covered by a test mocking the mailer.
116	> Task D: Session expiry revokes access on subsequent requests — covered by a test advancing time.
117	
118	Each task has one observable outcome, one test, and one commit boundary. That is a correctly sized task.
119	
120	**6. Order by dependency.**
121	Sequence tasks so that each one's dependencies are complete before it starts. Call out explicit dependencies (on other tasks in this list, on external work, on platform capabilities). If a task depends on work from another team, flag it immediately — Rule C4: dependencies are surfaced before commitment.
122	
123	**7. Write and file the task list.**
124	Use the template below. File at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`.
125	
126	## Task list template
127	
128	```markdown
129	# Tasks: <feature-slug>
130	
131	Design doc: docs/designs/<feature-slug>.md
132	Last updated: <date>
133	
134	## Task list
135	
136	### Task 1 — Walking skeleton
137	**Description:** [one sentence — what does the skeleton do?]
138	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
139	**Dependencies:** none
140	
141	### Task 2 — [name]
142	**Description:** [one sentence]
143	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
144	**Dependencies:** Task 1
145	
146	### Task N — [name]
147	**Description:** [one sentence]
148	**Done when:** [acceptance criterion]
149	**Dependencies:** [list, or "none"]
150	
151	## Open questions
152	<!-- Anything blocking or uncertain that was surfaced during breakdown.
153	     Do not start implementation until these are resolved. -->
154	```
155	
156	## Common rationalisations
157	
158	| Rationalisation | Rebuttal |
159	|---|---|
160	| "The tasks are obvious from the design doc — no need to write them out." | If they're obvious, writing them out takes 15 minutes. If it takes more, they weren't as obvious as assumed. The acceptance criteria are worth the time even if the tasks aren't. |
161	| "We'll figure out the order as we go." | Unordered tasks produce surprise dependencies mid-implementation. Dependency discovery during a build is expensive; discovery before the build is cheap. |
162	| "This task is a bit big but we know what we're doing." | Task size is not a trust issue — it is a verification issue. Big tasks fail silently. Small tasks fail loudly and early. |
163	| "The design doc isn't fully accepted but it's close enough." | An almost-accepted design doc means the approach is still negotiable. Tasks broken down from a negotiable design are likely to be redone. |
164	| "We don't need a walking skeleton — we'll integrate at the end." | Integration at the end is the most expensive integration there is. The walking skeleton exists to find integration problems on day 1, not day 29. |
165	| "We already know the dependencies — no need to list them." | Dependencies change during implementation; listing them before forces you to verify each one rather than discover them when they block you. A listed dependency that turns out not to exist costs nothing. An unlisted dependency that surfaces mid-sprint costs days. |
166	| "I'll write acceptance criteria after I implement so I know what 'done' looks like." | By then you've already built the wrong thing. Acceptance criteria written before implementation are a spec — they constrain what you build. Criteria written after implementation describe what you built. The goal is the former. |
167	| "The task list will need to change anyway, so there's no point spending time on it." | The value is not the list surviving implementation intact. It is the thinking done while creating it — discovering the dependencies, sizing the slices, identifying the walking skeleton. A plan that gets revised is still better than no plan. |
168	
169	## Red flags
170	
171	- The walking skeleton is missing or is listed as something other than task 1.
172	- Any task has more than one independent acceptance criterion.
173	- Acceptance criteria say "feature is implemented" or "code is written" rather than describing a verifiable condition.
174	- Acceptance criteria reference implementation details ("the function is written") rather than observable behaviour ("the endpoint returns X for input Y").
175	- The task list was written before reading the constraints section of the design doc.
176	- A task depends on unresolved external work that hasn't been flagged.
177	- The list was generated from the design doc introduction without reading the full document.
178	- A "setup" or "scaffolding" task appears before the walking skeleton, deferring integration discovery.
179	
180	## Verification / exit criteria
181	
182	The skill has run correctly when:
183	
184	1. A task list exists at `docs/tasks/<feature-slug>.md`.
185	2. Task 1 is the walking skeleton, with an acceptance criterion that can be checked independently of downstream tasks.
186	3. Every task has a single, concrete acceptance criterion stating a verifiable condition.
187	4. Every task can be described in one sentence.
188	5. Dependencies are listed for every task that has them.
189	6. Any external or cross-team dependency has been surfaced explicitly in the open questions section.
190	7. No "setup" or "scaffolding" task precedes the walking skeleton — if infrastructure is needed before the skeleton, the skeleton task encompasses it.
191	8. The open questions section is either populated with owners and resolution dates, or explicitly marked empty because no unresolved questions remain.
192	
193	## References
194	
195	- `rules/eng-principles-universal.md` — Rule A1 (design doc trigger), Rule B1 (YAGNI per slice), Rule B2 (walking skeleton first), Rule C4 (dependencies before commitment)
196	- `rules/eng-principles-agentic.md` — P3 (no drift from design doc)
197	- `skills/design-doc/SKILL.md` — the upstream output that this skill consumes
198	- Ryan Singer — "Shape Up" (appetite and scope as planning primitives)
199	
```

Here's the convergent shape before I write the plan:

**One reference file, two sections.** A 3-axis risk rubric (each axis Low/Medium/High with a concrete rubric per level, mirroring the Confidence Meter's table), then a deterministic routing table. The three axes are exactly your three factors:

| **Review load** | How costly is it for the operator to verify the artefact? | Where the operator spends review attention |

| **Domain complexity** | How likely is a missed failure case or scope drift? (ties to agentic P3) | Model tier + slice granularity / review |

**The reconciliation with P8** (the thing that makes this *belong* in your pack rather than fighting it): the file opens by stating this is **risk-weighted routing, not effort estimation**. It's a second lens layered on the existing Size check — verification-granularity says *"is this task small enough to verify in one slice?"* (a splitting rule); risk-sizing says *"given a correctly-sized task, how likely is it to go wrong and what model/review does it need?"* (a routing rule). They don't conflict — one bounds size, the other routes the bounded task.

**Model tier as a structured slot** so "Claude for now" doesn't rot: tiers are Fast / Balanced / Frontier, with one mapping line — `Fast = Haiku 4.5, Balanced = Sonnet 4.6, Frontier = Opus 4.7` — so a model bump is a one-line edit, and a future second vendor just adds a column.

1	# Benchmarks
2	
3	On a five-scenario benchmark of the [`plan-review`](../skills/plan-review/SKILL.md) skill on Claude Sonnet 4.6, with-skill runs catch **93%** of the issues a senior reviewer should catch. Baseline (the same model, same plan, no skill loaded) catches **19%**. Delta **+0.74**, stable across n=3.
4	
5	The number isn't the point on its own. The point is that without a skill, the model does what models do — it summarises the plan back. With the skill, it produces an explicit verdict that names the actual failure mode (no rollback, unverified scope claim, no problem statement). That difference is what calibration is for, and it's what this page tries to show with numbers and source links.
6	
7	Only `plan-review` has been calibrated against an eval set today. The methodology is reusable; benchmarking the other nine skills is on the roadmap.
8	
9	---
10	
11	## What we measured
12	
13	Five scenarios from [`skills/plan-review/evals/evals.json`](../skills/plan-review/evals/evals.json), each one a plan with at least one named class of defect that a senior reviewer should catch. Each eval has a set of binary assertions (e.g. "did the review classify Cynefin domain as Complicated", "did it produce verdict REVISE", "did it name a falsifying condition for the scope concern"). Pass rate = passed assertions / total assertions, averaged across n=3 runs per cell.
14	
15	The scenarios:
16	
17	1. **Roadmap without triage** — Allocate next quarter across three themes (shareable shortlist, push notifications, onboarding rewrite) with no triage records and no measurable outcomes. The defect: B1 problem framing missing, B3 confidence scoring missing, recommendation should be REVISE with idea-triage as the prerequisite.
18	2. **Vue component removal, unverified claim** — "Remove unused Vue components, starting with PrizeCategorySelector." The defect: "unused" is asserted, not verified; B2 scope verdict should be SUSTAINED with `grep` as the falsifying condition.
19	3. **Clean gem bump (the fast-track target)** — Bump rubocop 1.60 → 1.65, run tests, commit, 30 minutes. The shape: Cynefin Clear, Quick tier, fully reversible — should fast-track to APPROVE without inflating into Full-tier review. The skill must classify and downgrade, not invent concerns to look thorough.
20	4. **Schema migration without rollback** — Add NOT NULL column to a 50M-row users table, backfill during deploy. The defect: B5 reversibility (no ADR, no alternatives), B6 operability (no rollback, no backfill metrics, no capacity headroom). Recommendation should be REVISE.
21	5. **Redis cache, solutionism** — Introduce Redis caching for catalog reads. The defect: solution-first framing — no problem statement, no latency target. B1 SUSTAINED, B6 SUSTAINED, REVISE.
22	
23	Together they cover the most common plan-review failure surfaces: missing problem statements, scope inflation, unverified assertions, irreversibility hidden behind plain language, and solutionism.
24	
25	---
26	
27	## How we measured
28	
29	**n=3 per cell.** Each (eval × condition) cell is run three times. We report the mean and standard deviation so the reader can see the run-to-run variance, not just a single point.
30	
31	**Two conditions.**
32	- *With skill*: the runner has the [`plan-review`](../skills/plan-review/SKILL.md) `SKILL.md` loaded.
33	- *Baseline*: the runner gets the same plan, on the same model, with no skill in context — the closest analogue to "what generic prompting produces."
34	
35	**Isolated grader.** A separate agent grades each review. The grader is blinded to which condition produced the review, has no access to `SKILL.md`, and operates under strict rules: assertions only PASS with quotable evidence; bucket verdicts must be explicit (e.g. literal "B5 SUSTAINED", not paraphrased); Cynefin domain and tier must be named, not inferred.
36	
37	**Why isolated grading matters.** In iter-3 we discovered the grader (which had visibility into adjacent metadata files in the workspace) was inflating baseline scores — apparent baseline jumped from 42% to 92% across one iteration with no SKILL.md change. Switching to an isolated, blinded grader in iter-4 dropped the apparent baseline back to 36%. The +0.54 delta from iter-4 onward is the first trustworthy number this benchmark produced. Catching that measurement bug is what calibration is for.
38	
39	---
40	
41	## Results
42	
43	Iter-5, the latest run. Claude Sonnet 4.6 on both runner and grader. n=3 per cell.
44	
45	| Eval | With skill | Without skill | Delta |
46	|---|---|---|---|
47	| 1 — roadmap without triage | 81% ± 6% | 4% ± 6% | **+77pp** |
48	| 2 — vue removal, unverified claim | 94% ± 10% | 22% ± 10% | **+72pp** |
49	| 3 — clean gem bump | 100% ± 0% | 11% ± 10% | **+89pp** |
50	| 4 — schema migration, no rollback | 100% ± 0% | 25% ± 0% | **+75pp** |
51	| 5 — redis cache, solutionism | 90% ± 8% | 33% ± 8% | **+57pp** |
52	| **Aggregate** | **93% ± 2%** | **19% ± 5%** | **+0.74** |
53	
54	Every scenario shows a large delta. The smallest (eval-5, +57pp) is still substantial. The biggest (eval-3, +89pp) is the calibration target the skill was tuned to handle correctly — not over-reviewing a 30-minute reversible change.
55	
56	---
57	
58	## What the skill is actually doing differently
59	
60	The number is one thing. The texture of the difference is another. Three things show up consistently across runs.
61	
62	**Naming the failure mode, not just describing the plan.** Baseline reviews tend to summarise the plan back and add a few generic concerns. With-skill reviews produce explicit verdicts attached to specific buckets. On the schema-migration eval, baseline produces text like "this looks risky, consider a rollback plan." With-skill produces "B6 SUSTAINED — no rollback path named, no backfill progress metric, no capacity headroom analysis on a 50M-row backfill." The grader's strict literal-evidence rule rewards this; it's also what a senior reviewer would write.
63	
64	**Classifying the work before reviewing it.** The skill's first step is Cynefin classification + tier (Quick or Full). This is what fixed eval-3 from 72% to 100% — explicit `Cynefin: Clear` and `Tier: Quick` labels in the fast-track output template (added between iter-4 and iter-5, commit `440a15f`). Baseline rarely classifies anything; it reviews every plan as though it were the same shape, which is how 30-minute gem bumps end up with the same review template as 50M-row schema migrations.
65	
66	**Catching one-way doors.** Two of the five evals (schema migration, vue removal) hide irreversibility behind plain language. Baseline accepts both; with-skill names the falsifying condition that should overturn the verdict — `grep PrizeCategorySelector` for the "unused component" claim, "no `docs/adr/*.md` for this migration" for the schema change. This is the bucket of work that's most expensive to get wrong in production, and it's where the delta is largest.
67	
68	---
69	
70	## The trajectory
71	
72	Five iterations.
73	
74	- **Iter-1 / iter-2**: drafted the SKILL.md, found a calibration defect on the fast-track eval (eval-3, baseline-ish behaviour: skill kept inflating a 30-min reversible change into Full-tier review).
75	- **Iter-3**: added the Step 1a fast-track gate. Baseline appeared to jump to 92% — suspicious. Investigation showed the self-grader was reading adjacent `grading.json` files in the workspace, contaminating its judgement.
76	- **Iter-4**: switched to an isolated, blinded grader with no workspace metadata access and strict literal-evidence rules. Baseline collapsed back to 36%. Aggregate delta **+0.54** — the first trustworthy number this benchmark produced.
77	- **Iter-5**: closed the eval-3 gap (72% → 100%) by making `Cynefin domain:` and `Tier:` lines mandatory in the fast-track output template. Re-ran the full sweep on Sonnet 4.6 (≈30% cheaper than Opus 4.7). Aggregate delta **+0.74**, stable across n=3.
78	
79	Each iteration's analysis lives in [`skills/plan-review-workspace/iteration-{1..5}/benchmark.md`](../skills/plan-review-workspace/).
80	
81	---
82	
83	## Caveats
84	
85	**Model swap on iter-5.** Iter-4 was Opus 4.7 on both runner and grader; iter-5 was Sonnet 4.6 on both. The +0.74 vs iter-4's +0.54 numbers are not apples-to-apples — the sonnet baseline produces less structured output (without-skill score dropped 36% → 19%), which the strict grader penalises more heavily. The clean claim from iter-5 is "the iter-5 SKILL.md, run on sonnet, produces a +0.74 lift over a sonnet baseline." Running the iter-4 SKILL.md on sonnet (a same-model comparison) would isolate the SKILL.md improvement from the model change. That comparison is an open item.
86	
87	**One skill calibrated.** The other nine skills in this pack haven't been benchmarked yet. The methodology — eval scenarios with binary assertions, isolated blinded grader, n=3 per cell — is reusable, and benchmarking is on the roadmap. Today the +0.74 number speaks for `plan-review` specifically.
88	
89	**Runner phrasing variance.** Eval-1 and eval-5 dropped slightly with-skill in iter-5 (93% → 81% and 100% → 90%). Inspection of the failing assertions shows runner phrasing differences ("REWORK" or "DO NOT APPROVE" instead of literal "REVISE"; bucket reasoning correct but missing the explicit "B1 SUSTAINED" label) tripping the grader's strict literal-matching rule. These aren't skill-content gaps; they're a candidate target for a future SKILL.md tightening pass on the output template's literal-string requirements.
90	
91	**Five scenarios is not a comprehensive surface.** The scenarios were chosen to cover representative failure modes (Cynefin classification, fast-track gate, irreversibility, missing rollback, solutionism). They don't cover every plan shape a reader might bring. Adding scenarios is cheap; they're plain JSON in `evals.json`.
92	
93	**This is one number, not a leaderboard.** The benchmark is here to answer "does the skill make the agent better at the task it's meant to do." It's not a comparison across models, prompting strategies, or competing skill packs.
94	
95	---
96	
97	## Reproducibility
98	
99	| Artefact | Path |
100	|---|---|
101	| Eval definitions (5 scenarios, assertions) | [`skills/plan-review/evals/evals.json`](../skills/plan-review/evals/evals.json) |
102	| Per-iteration analysis writeups | [`skills/plan-review-workspace/iteration-{1..5}/benchmark.md`](../skills/plan-review-workspace/) |
103	| Per-iteration machine-readable results | `skills/plan-review-workspace/iteration-{1..5}/benchmark.json` |
104	| Aggregator scripts (n=3 stats, iter comparison) | `skills/plan-review-workspace/iteration-{3,4,5}/aggregate.py` |
105	| Iter-4 grader prompt (isolated grader rules) | `skills/plan-review-workspace/iteration-4/grader-prompt.md` |
106	
107	Raw per-cell run data (60+ runner outputs and grading files per iteration) is gitignored — workspace bloat with no durable analytical value. The analysis files (`benchmark.md`, `benchmark.json`, `aggregate.py`, `grader-prompt.md`) are in git and tell the full story across iterations.
108

1	# Sub-agent persona anatomy
2	
3	The authoring spec for sub-agent personas in `agents/<name>/`.
4	
5	Sub-agent personas are review passes invoked with fresh context. They are the right tool when judgement is required and the original agent has structural reasons not to catch its own failure (it claimed success, it picked the exciting tech, it solved the easier problem). They are the wrong tool for mechanical checks (use a hook) or for general guidance (embed the principle in the relevant skill).
6	
7	## Frontmatter
8	
9	```yaml
10	---
11	name: <agent-name>
12	description: <1-2 sentences>
13	category: sub-agent
14	pack: <product | engineering | meta>
15	review_target: <design-doc | implementation | spec | other>
16	principles_implemented:
17	  - source: <eng-universal | eng-agentic | roadmap | product>
18	    id: <principle or rule id>
19	    bucket: sub-agent
20	length_target: <e.g. 150-200>
21	author: Anton Babushkin
22	predecessor:
23	  repo: <URL or "none">
24	  skill: <skill name in source repo, or "none">
25	  relation: <derivative | adjacent | new>
26	kept_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
27	changed_from_predecessor: <list, or "n/a">
28	---
29	```
30	
31	## Sections
32	
33	In this exact order:
34	
35	1. **Title** — `# <Persona Name>`
36	2. **Purpose** — one paragraph: what this review catches and why the original agent can't
37	3. **Review posture** — the stance this persona takes (adversarial, fresh-eyes, no investment in the original work). Names the rationalisations the original agent has structural reasons to miss.
38	4. **Context to load** — what files, ADRs, or specs the persona reads *before* seeing the original agent's output. The point is to form an independent view first, then check it against the work.
39	5. **Trigger** — when this review fires
40	6. **Inputs** — what the persona is given
41	7. **Outputs** — review verdict (`accept`, `accept with notes`, `reject`) and structured notes
42	8. **Workflow** — numbered steps the persona walks through
43	9. **Common rationalisations** — patterns the *original* agent will use that this persona is on the lookout for
44	10. **Red flags** — signals that warrant a `reject`
45	11. **Out of scope** — what other artefacts cover; this persona's specific lane
46	12. **References** — principles enforced
47	
48	## Length
49	
50	Target 150–200 lines. Hard cap 250.
51	
52	## Reject triggers specific to sub-agents
53	
54	- Missing the "Review posture" section.
55	- Missing the "Context to load" section, or context loading happens after seeing the original work (this pollutes the independent view).
56	- The persona's lane overlaps another sub-agent's without an explicit `Out of scope` boundary.
57	- The persona is a thin wrapper around an existing skill rather than a genuine adversarial second pass.
58	- The persona's review verdict criteria reduce to "does this match my interpretation" — should be principle-grounded.
59	
60	## See also
61	
62	- `docs/skill-anatomy.md`, `docs/hook-anatomy.md`
63	- `docs/brief-template.md`
64	- `docs/authoring-learnings.md`
65	
```

Research complete. Here is the structured report.

---

# Model-Tier Selection at Planning Time (Haiku / Sonnet / Opus)

A reference grounded in Anthropic's own documentation as the primary source, with practitioner/industry sources for the dimensions Anthropic doesn't cover. Every claim carries a URL. Items I could not verify against a source are flagged as **[INFERENCE]**.

---

## 1. Anthropic's official model-selection guidance

Anthropic has two relevant docs: the **Models overview** (positioning + spec table) and a dedicated **Choosing the right model** page (the decision framework). Both are current as of May 2026 and cover Opus 4.7 / Sonnet 4.6 / Haiku 4.5.

### Tier positioning (verbatim, from the Models overview comparison table)

| | Claude Opus 4.7 | Claude Sonnet 4.6 | Claude Haiku 4.5 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Description** | "Our most capable generally available model for complex reasoning and agentic coding" | "The best combination of speed and intelligence" | "The fastest model with near-frontier intelligence" |
| **Pricing** | $5 / $25 per MTok | $3 / $15 per MTok | $1 / $5 per MTok |
| **Comparative latency** | Moderate | Fast | Fastest |
| **Context window** | 1M tokens | 1M tokens | 200k tokens |
| **Max output** | 128k | 64k | 64k |

The overview's explicit default: *"If you're unsure which model to use, consider starting with **Claude Opus 4.7** for the most complex tasks. It is our most capable generally available model, with a step-change improvement in agentic coding over Claude Opus 4.6."*

Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/overview

### The decision framework (verbatim, from "Choosing the right model")

Anthropic frames the choice as **balancing three considerations**:
- **Capabilities** — "What specific features or capabilities will you need the model to have in order to meet your needs?"
- **Speed** — "How quickly does the model need to respond in your application?"
- **Cost** — "What's your budget for both development and production usage?"

It then gives **two starting strategies**:

- **Option 1 — Start with a fast, cost-effective model** (Haiku 4.5): "begin implementation… test thoroughly… evaluate if performance meets your requirements… upgrade only if necessary for specific capability gaps." Best for: *initial prototyping, applications with tight latency requirements, cost-sensitive implementations, high-volume straightforward tasks.*
- **Option 2 — Start with the most capable model** (Opus 4.7): "implement… optimize your prompts… evaluate… consider increasing efficiency by downgrading intelligence over time." Best for: *complex reasoning, scientific/mathematical applications, tasks requiring nuanced understanding, applications where accuracy outweighs cost, advanced coding.*

Selection matrix (verbatim example use-cases):
- **Opus 4.7** — "Long-horizon agentic coding, large-scale refactoring, complex systems engineering, advanced research, multi-hour autonomous tasks."
- **Sonnet 4.6** — "Code generation, data analysis, content creation, visual understanding, agentic tool use."
- **Haiku 4.5** — "Real-time applications, high-volume intelligent processing, cost-sensitive deployments needing strong reasoning, sub-agent tasks."

How to decide to upgrade/change (verbatim, abbreviated): *"(1) Create benchmark tests specific to your use case — having a good evaluation set is the most important step… (2) Test with your actual prompts and data… (3) Compare performance across models for accuracy, response quality, handling of edge cases… (4) Weigh performance and cost tradeoffs."*

Source: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/docs/about-claude/models/choosing-a-model

**Key takeaway for your doc:** Anthropic's official position is explicitly **two-directional** — you can default-up (Opus, then optimize down) or default-down (Haiku, then escalate on capability gaps). Their core axes are exactly three: **capabilities, speed, cost.** Everything else below is practitioner elaboration on what "capabilities" decomposes into.

---

## 2. The dimensions that actually drive the choice

Combining Anthropic's three core axes with practitioner/industry writing on coding/agentic model selection. For each: definition, push direction, source.

| Dimension | One-line definition | Pushes toward… | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Task complexity / reasoning depth** | Degree of multi-step reasoning, planning, and error recovery required (vs. classification/extraction/formatting). | **Stronger** model as complexity rises. | Anthropic ("complex reasoning") [overview]; "Low complexity covers classification, extraction, formatting, simple Q&A" — Wilkins; "task's domain and difficulty" — Augment |
| **Cost per token / volume** | Per-token price differential × task frequency. | **Cheaper** model as volume rises. | Anthropic ("cost") [choosing-a-model]; "Routing Opus 4.6 to file navigation can increase input token costs ~5x vs Haiku 4.5" — Augment; "processing 10,000 tickets through Opus when Haiku would do, you're burning money" — Wilkins |
| **Latency sensitivity** | Acceptable response time; interactive vs. async. | **Faster** model when interactive; tolerate slower for async depth. | Anthropic ("speed") [choosing-a-model]; "the difference between 200ms and 2 seconds changes the UX entirely" — Wilkins; "waiting 30s is preferable for async code review if it catches subtle bugs" — Augment |
| **Context window need** | Volume of files/instructions that must fit in one window. | **Larger-context** model (note Haiku is 200k vs 1M for Sonnet/Opus). | Anthropic [overview table]; "A 4K context window can't hold a meaningful codebase" — Wilkins |
| **Stakes / cost-of-error / blast radius** | Financial, reputational, or downstream-cascade damage from a wrong output. | **Stronger** model + human-in-loop as stakes rise. | "If a mistake costs $100,000 or a customer relationship, keep humans in the loop" — Wilkins; "every downstream agent operates on malformed inputs with no upstream correction" — Augment |
| **Agentic tool-use / long-horizon orchestration** | Multi-step autonomous execution with tool calls, planning loops, self-correction; or coordinating sub-agents. | **Stronger** model for the orchestrator/long-horizon role. | Anthropic ("long-horizon agentic coding, multi-hour autonomous tasks" for Opus) [choosing-a-model]; orchestrator-Opus / worker-Sonnet pattern — Anthropic multi-agent post |
| **Ambiguity / specification completeness** | Whether inputs/outputs are predetermined vs. variable and requiring judgment. | **Stronger** model when underspecified. | "Automation breaks when inputs deviate… it can't handle ambiguity" — Wilkins; reasoning models "pause-and-ask when a quick guess could send you miles in the wrong direction" — skywork |
| **Hallucination / factual-precision sensitivity** | Cost of plausible-but-wrong fabrication in this domain. | **Stronger** model; avoid small models for judgment. | "Smaller models have less internal knowledge… they fill gaps with plausible-sounding fabrication" — Wilkins; "lowest-hallucination models ideal for legal, healthcare, finance, regulated domains" — Bizzmark |
| **Tool-call frequency** | Number of API/tool invocations per task (efficiency cost). | **Efficient** model for high-frequency loops. | "switching Sonnet 4.0→4.5 produced 34% fewer tool calls on average" — Augment |

Sources: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/overview · https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/docs/about-claude/models/choosing-a-model · https://www.augmentcode.com/guides/ai-model-routing-guide · https://collinwilkins.com/articles/ai-model-selection.html · https://bizzmarkblog.com/selecting-models-for-high-stakes-production-using-aa-omniscience-to-measure-and-manage-hallucination-risk/ · https://skywork.ai/blog/ai-agent/gpt-5-1-thinking-ambiguity-handling-explained/

---

## 3. Model-routing / escalation patterns in agentic systems

Two distinct documented patterns, and the distinction is exactly the planning-vs-runtime one you care about:

- **Routing** — an **upfront, one-shot pre-classification**: "before executing a query, a router classifies it and sends it to exactly one model." Three classification approaches: intent classification, complexity estimation, semantic similarity to historical queries. This is a **planning-time** decision.
- **Cascading** — **runtime escalation**: "the query first goes to the cheapest model. If that model's output confidence is below a threshold, the query escalates to the next tier." The escalation signal is the small model's **uncertainty** (token-probability spread), with refinements like early abstention ("the model explicitly signals capability limits rather than guessing") and empirically calibrated thresholds. This is a **runtime** decision.

Reported economics: routing can deliver "cost reductions of 45–85%… while maintaining 95% of frontier model quality by routing 85% of queries to cheaper models"; the academic survey of cascades reports "26% average cost reduction with up to 70% in optimal cases."

**Crucial point for a planning-time doc:** coding-agent practitioners explicitly favor **static (pre-classification) routing** over runtime escalation for predictable workloads — *"Static routing works best for teams with predictable workloads where task types map cleanly to agent roles"* (Augment). Anthropic's own production multi-agent system uses **static role-based assignment**: **Opus as orchestrator, Sonnet as worker sub-agents**, which "outperformed single-agent Opus by 90.2%." Their cost analysis found "token usage by itself explains 80% of the variance" in performance, with tool-call count and model choice as the other two factors — and that multi-agent is only worth it "where the value of the task is high enough to pay for the increased performance." So the orchestration-tier decision is treated as a planning-time architecture choice, not a runtime one.

Routing is pre-classification; cascading is runtime escalation — and the literature treats them as combinable (a unified framework exists: arXiv 2410.10347).

Sources: https://tianpan.co/blog/2025-11-03-llm-routing-model-cascades · https://www.augmentcode.com/guides/ai-model-routing-guide · https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system · https://arxiv.org/pdf/2410.10347 · https://blog.meganova.ai/the-3-tier-routing-cascade-rule-based-semantic-llm/

---

## 4. Which dimensions are knowable at PLANNING time vs only at RUNTIME

This is the central distinction for your use case. Sorted:

**Estimable at planning time** (you can annotate the task before it runs):
- **Cost budget / expected volume** — a property of the workload, known upfront.
- **Latency SLA** — a property of where the output is consumed (interactive vs. async), known upfront.
- **Stakes / cost-of-error / blast radius** — known from what the task touches (prod data, auth, public API, throwaway script).
- **Context size** — estimable from the files/codebase the task must reason over.
- **Specification completeness** — you can read the task and judge whether it's well-specified or ambiguous. This is the **planning-time proxy** for the runtime quantity "actual ambiguity encountered." It's the one practitioners use because it's the only ambiguity signal available before execution.
- **Agentic / long-horizon orchestration** — you decide the *architecture* (single call vs. orchestrator+sub-agents) at planning time; Anthropic assigns tiers to roles statically.
- **Expected task complexity** — an **estimate** at planning time (this is what "complexity estimation" routing classifiers do), but only an estimate.

**Only observable at runtime:**
- **Actual difficulty** — whether the task turned out harder than estimated. (Routing classifiers *estimate* this; cascades *measure* it.)
- **Whether the model got stuck / low confidence** — the core cascade escalation signal (token-probability spread, early abstention). Inherently runtime.
- **Actual tool-call count / loop length** — known only as the agent executes.
- **Actual hallucination incidence** — you can estimate *sensitivity* (domain) at planning time, but whether a fabrication actually occurred is runtime/post-hoc.

**[INFERENCE]** The clean implication for your doc: a planning-time annotation can only ever set a **prior** (via routing-style classification on the estimable dimensions). The runtime-only dimensions are what a *cascade/escalation* layer would catch later. If your doc is purely planning-time, lean on the estimable set and treat complexity as an estimate that may be revised — don't pretend you can know "did it get stuck" in advance. This sorting follows directly from the routing-vs-cascade distinction in §3, which the sources establish; the assignment of each specific dimension to a bucket is my synthesis.

---

## 5. Critique of your three invented criteria

Your three: **(a) operator cognitive load to review the output, (b) hallucination/mistake risk, (c) domain complexity causing failure cases or scope drift.**

**(b) Hallucination/mistake risk** — **Maps directly** to a real, well-documented dimension (the "hallucination / factual-precision sensitivity" axis, and overlaps with "stakes/cost-of-error"). Sources are explicit that this pushes toward stronger models and away from small ones (Wilkins, Bizzmark). Keep it. Knowable at planning time *as sensitivity* (domain), not as actual incidence.

**(c) Domain complexity → failure cases / scope drift** — **Maps** to a blend of two documented dimensions: **task complexity/reasoning depth** (Anthropic's "capabilities" axis) and **ambiguity/specification completeness** ("automation breaks when inputs deviate… can't handle ambiguity" — Wilkins). "Scope drift" specifically is the agentic-horizon concern (long-horizon autonomous tasks are where drift happens — Anthropic's Opus matrix row). It's a reasonable composite but it's actually **three** documented axes fused into one — you may want to split complexity, ambiguity, and horizon, since they're estimable independently at planning time.

**(a) Operator cognitive load to review** — **Partial map, and the weakest of the three.** I could **not find this named as a model-selection dimension** in Anthropic's docs or the practitioner routing guides. The closest documented support: (i) Anthropic's agentic-engineering principles note that *review matters more* under agents because a human can't review 1000 lines/hour at the same proportion they review 100 — but that's an argument about *review process*, not about *picking a model tier*; and (ii) "keep humans in the loop" for high-stakes work (Wilkins) ties review burden to **stakes**, not to a standalone axis. **[INFERENCE]** Operator review load is better modeled as a *downstream consequence* of stakes + output volume + hallucination risk than as an independent input dimension. If you keep it, ground it in stakes; don't present it as a documented primary criterion.

### Real dimensions MISSING from your set of three

Your three omit several documented, **planning-time-knowable** dimensions — notably the ones in **Anthropic's own core triad**:

1. **Cost / volume** — Anthropic's third core axis; entirely absent from your set. A high-volume task is the single most common reason to route *down*, regardless of complexity.
2. **Latency / speed** — Anthropic's second core axis; absent. Interactive vs. async is a first-order planning-time signal.
3. **Context window size** — absent; a hard constraint (Haiku caps at 200k vs. 1M), and a planning-time-knowable one.
4. **Agentic long-horizon / orchestration role** — absent as an explicit axis; this is the dimension Anthropic's production system routes on most deliberately (orchestrator=Opus, worker=Sonnet).

**Bottom line:** Of your three, (b) is solid and well-sourced, (c) is real but conflates three separable axes, and (a) is not a documented model-selection criterion (it's a derived consequence of stakes). The biggest gap is that your set leans entirely on *quality/risk* dimensions and omits the *economics/constraints* half of Anthropic's own framework — cost, latency, and context — all of which are knowable at planning time and all of which routinely override a complexity-based choice.

The research came back grounded, and it meaningfully reshapes the idea. Here's what matters.

## The headline reframe

**Anthropic's own decision framework is a three-part triad: capabilities, speed, cost.** Everything else decomposes from "capabilities." Their docs give two strategies — *default-up* (start Opus, optimize down) or *default-down* (start Haiku, escalate on capability gaps) — and a static role-assignment pattern: in their production multi-agent system, **Opus orchestrates, Sonnet does the worker sub-tasks**, beating single-Opus by 90%. ([overview](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/models/overview), [choosing-a-model](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/docs/about-claude/models/choosing-a-model))

**Two patterns, and the distinction is exactly your use case:** *routing* = upfront pre-classification (planning-time — one model picked before the task runs); *cascading* = runtime escalation (start cheap, escalate when the model signals low confidence). A sizing annotation written during planning is **routing**. Cascading is a separate runtime layer — out of scope for a planning-time reference, but worth naming.

## Your three criteria, judged against the sources

| Your criterion | Verdict |
|---|---|
| **Hallucination/mistake risk** | ✅ **Solid.** Maps to a documented "factual-precision sensitivity" axis; ties directly to your own agentic P2. Keep it. |
| **Domain complexity → failure/drift** | ⚠️ **Real but conflated.** It's actually *three* separable, independently-estimable axes fused: reasoning complexity + specification ambiguity + long-horizon/scope. |
| **Operator review load** | ❌ **Not a documented model-selection criterion.** No source treats it as an *input* to model choice. It's a *derived consequence* of stakes + output volume + hallucination risk. |

That last point is the important correction: **review load is an output, not an input.** Which actually makes your "both equally" answer cleaner than before — the model tier *and* the "where the operator should look" flag both fall out of the *same* underlying axes. Review load doesn't need to be scored separately; it's computed.

## What's missing from your three

The biggest gap: your set is all *quality/risk* and omits the *constraints* half of Anthropic's own triad — **cost/volume, latency, context size** — plus the **orchestration role** (single bounded slice = "worker" → cheaper; multi-step orchestration → Opus) that Anthropic routes on most deliberately.

But here's the honest scoping for *this* pack: tasks coming out of `planning-and-task-breakdown` are already bounded to one verifiable slice, and they're async dev work, not interactive high-volume serving. So **latency and per-task volume are mostly constant/low-relevance here** — I'd name them as dimensions and explicitly set them aside with that rationale, rather than pretend they don't exist or force them in.

Two things also clicked into place with existing pack concepts:

- **Stakes/reversibility = one-way doors**, which `plan-review` *already* classifies (Cynefin + one-way-door detection, universal P3). The model-routing reference can reuse that vocabulary instead of inventing.
- **Orchestrator/worker = your skills + sub-agent personas.** `sub-agent-anatomy.md` already allows a `model:` field (and `entire-search.md` uses `model: haiku`). The "worker sub-agent → cheaper tier" pattern is already latent in the pack.

## Proposed refined criteria set (for this pack, planning-time)

**Active axes** (each estimable before the task runs, each Low/Med/High):
1. **Reasoning complexity** — mechanical edit vs. multi-step reasoning
2. **Specification completeness** — is the task a clear one-liner with one acceptance criterion (which planning-and-task-breakdown already forces), or underspecified?
3. **Hallucination sensitivity** — novel/version-specific API surface → also triggers `source-driven-development`
4. **Stakes / reversibility** — one-way door, prod data, auth, schema, public API → strongest tier + human review
5. **Orchestration role** — bounded worker slice vs. orchestrating/long-horizon task

**Explicitly set aside** (named, with rationale): cost/volume, latency, context size.

**Derived outputs:** model tier (from axes 1–5) + review-attention flag (from stakes + complexity + hallucination).

complexity or orchestration High →\n     prefer smaller slices / more gates.\n\n7. **Annotation format** — the compact one-liner appended to each task so \"every task gets\n   sized\" is real without a template-field change, e.g.:\n   `Model: Opus 4.7 (Frontier) · complexity=H spec=M halluc=H stakes=H(one-way) orch=L · +source-driven-development · review: flag`\n\n8. **Worked example** — score one sample task across the five axes and derive tier + flag\n   (mirrors `ice-scoring.md`'s worked example).\n\n9. **Common miscalibrations** — e.g. scoring review-load as an input; defaulting up on\n   reversible work; treating a well-specified task as complex.\n\n10. **What this is not for** — not effort/time estimation; not a runtime cascade/escalation\n    mechanism; not a replacement for the verification-granularity Size check.\n\n11. **Sources** — Anthropic overview + choosing-a-model + multi-agent post; > AGENT

Understood — that reframes the deliverable. You don't want an implementation plan for the markdown file; you want this captured **in Linear** as a thoroughly-shaped plan, so the routing layer gets built comprehensively when you're ready (and so it's the kind of thing that itself gets routing-layer annotations once it exists). The design research I just did isn't wasted — it becomes the *substance* that makes the Linear plan thorough.

You flagged the open question yourself: whole project or a single task? Let me resolve that before I rewrite, because it changes the structure.

My read, against your own `linear-workflow.md` size table: the comprehensive vision is **6–8 issues** — the reference rubric, threading it into `planning-and-task-breakdown`, template fields, threading into `initiative-shape`/`roadmap-shape`, an enforcement hook, and the sub-agent `model:` convention. That's 5+ issues with a clear goal → **initiative**, not a single task. A single task would force all of that into one undifferentiated lump, which is exactly what the initiative model exists to prevent.

Grounded in universal P3.\n- Must not fight agentic **P8** — framed as risk-weighted routing, *not* effort estimation;\n  a second lens on the existing verification-granularity Size check.\n\n## How the initiative gets created\n\nPer `rules/linear-workflow.md`: **do not hand-create Linear projects for goal-directed\nwork** — run **`/initiative-shape`**, which enforces the six-field check and the 11-rule\nverification gate (Step 6.5). 0 tasks carry a model/risk note\n        target:   100% of tasks in a sample run carry the annotation\n        window:   by cycle close\n        source:   skills/planning-and-task-breakdown/SKILL.md + a sample list under docs/tasks/\n\n  KR3 (commit) [brake] — the routing is deterministic: two independent applications of the\n      rubric to the same task agree on (tier, review-flag).\n        baseline: model choice is non-reproducible (no rubric)\n        target:   2 independent runs agree on tier + flag for the worked example + 1 held-out task\n        window:   by cycle close\n        source:   the worked example in the reference + a second sample task\n\nAffected repos: agent-skills-shaper  (artefact lives here; governs tasks across all repos)\nAppetite:       ~7 issues\nKill condition: applying the rubric to real tasks doesn't change the model you'd have picked\n                anyway (no decision value), OR the annotation is routinely skipped in practice\n                (ceremony without behaviour change).\nProject type:   1 (methodology) — confirm vs 3 (utility skill pack) during shaping\n```\n\nA 4th, aspirational KR (comprehensive reach) is a candidate: *the layer is threaded beyond\nplanning (initiative-shape / roadmap reference it) and a hook flags un-annotated tasks.*\nLet `/initiative-shape` decide whether to include it (it wants 3–5 KRs).\n\n## Draft issue breakdown (~7 issues — fills the appetite)\n\nCaptured now so the parked initiative is thorough.
